- Emotion: Smooth, luminous gradients evoke atmospheric depth, sky/horizon naturalism, and modern tactile polish.
- Attention: Soft vignette or directional gradients subtly steer gaze toward the brighter focal region without hard geometric borders.
- Production value: Visible 8-bit contour stair-stepping (Mach banding) and muddy gray midpoints in gradients are the single most common "cheap tell" in digital video production.
- Clarity: Preserves clean background fields behind typography without distracting contour rings.
- Cost: Fragment-shader OKLab interpolation + blue-noise dithering costs `< 0.1 ms` per frame at `4K` while saving massive bitrate and eliminating re-renders.
  Rules:

1. Never interpolate gradients between distant hues ($\Delta h > 60^\circ$) directly in display-encoded sRGB: sRGB nonlinear interpolation passes through a desaturated, darkened "muddy gray dead zone" at $t = 0.5$. Always interpolate color stops in **OKLab** (for smooth Cartesian transitions) or **OKLCH** (for vibrant chromatic arcs), or in **Scene-Linear RGB** when simulating physical light falloff ([Ottosson, Oklab](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fbottosson.github.io%2Fposts%2Foklab%2F)).
2. Calculate the **Banding Risk Ratio**: when a gradient spans $D$ pixels across the screen with a total 8-bit digital code value delta of $\Delta V_{8\text{bit}}$ (where $V \in [0, 255]$), visible Mach banding is guaranteed whenever $\frac{D}{\Delta V_{8\text{bit}}} > 4.0\text{ pixels/step}$ (e.g., a subtle dark background gradient from `#141820` to `#202632` spanning `1920 px` has $\Delta V \approx 14$, giving $137\text{ px/step}$).
3. Eliminate Mach banding using two mandatory pipeline defenses:
   - Render and composite all gradients in `16-bit half-float` (`R16G16B16A16_SFLOAT`) or `10-bit/12-bit` integer buffers prior to final output encoding.
   - Apply **Triangular Probability Density Function (TPDF) Blue-Noise Dithering** with an amplitude of `±0.5 to ±1.0 LSB` (in target 8-bit/10-bit code values, i.e., `±1/255` for 8-bit or `±1/1023` for 10-bit) plus subtle monochromatic film grain (`0.8%–1.5%` RMS amplitude) before video compression so H.264/H.265 macroblock quantization does not re-introduce banding ([Vulkan Format Spec](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fdocs.vulkan.org%2Fspec%2Flatest%2Findex.html); [DaVinci Resolve Manual](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fdocuments.blackmagicdesign.com%2FUserManuals%2FDaVinci-Resolve-20-Beginners-Guide.pdf%3F_v%3D1757574013000)).
4. Space intermediate gradient stops along an eased curve (`cubic-in-out` or `sine-in-out`) rather than linear `0% -> 100%` stop clamping; linear stop boundaries have a discontinuous first derivative ($C^0$ only), which creates a perceived bright or dark crease at the start and end of the ramp (Mach band illusion) ([Albers, Interaction of Color](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%2Fabout%2FInteraction_of_Color.html%3Fid%3DA_MYU_XDXfcC)).
   Event mapping: Planar shader uniform `gradient_descriptor(type="linear"|"radial"|"conic"|"mesh_patch", space="oklab"|"oklch"|"linear_rgb", stops=[(t, L, C, h)...], ease_curve="cubic_in_out", dither_lsb=1.0)`.
   Raster/bake note: Evaluate linear, radial, and conic gradients procedurally in the fragment shader in `fp16` precision with tiled `64×64` blue-noise texture lookup; for complex 2D mesh/patch gradients (Hermite/Coons patches), tessellate the patch with `≥ 32×32` subdivisions and interpolate vertex colors in OKLab in the fragment stage.
   Cultural notes: Vibrant multi-point mesh gradients ("aurora gradients" in OKLCH) define contemporary digital product branding, whereas subtle single-hue linear vignettes define classical narrative cinema grading.
   Failure modes: Rendering a dark vignette in an 8-bit buffer without dithering → concentric "onion-ring" contour bands pulse across the background during video playback; interpolating Blue (`#0044FF`) to Yellow (`#FFEE00`) in sRGB → a dull brownish-gray smear appears in the middle of the ramp.
   Checklist: [ ] Gradient interpolation space set to `oklab`, `oklch`, or `linear_rgb` (never raw sRGB for $\Delta h > 60^\circ$) [ ] Compositing buffer is `≥ 16-bit float` [ ] Blue-noise TPDF dithering (`±1.0 LSB`) applied before quantization [ ] Gradient stop falloff eased ($C^1$ continuous) to prevent Mach crease
   Sources: [Ottosson, Oklab](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fbottosson.github.io%2Fposts%2Foklab%2F); [Albers, Interaction of Color](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%2Fabout%2FInteraction_of_Color.html%3Fid%3DA_MYU_XDXfcC); [Vulkan Documentation](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fdocs.vulkan.org%2Fspec%2Flatest%2Findex.html).

## 5. Color Pipeline

### 5.1 Scene-Referred vs. Display-Referred Linear Workflow and ACES/OCIO Concepts

Definition: A deterministic end-to-end color management architecture that converts all input assets via Input Transforms (IDTs) into a wide-gamut, scene-linear working space (`[0, +∞)`) for physically plausible lighting, blurring, and compositing, and maps the result to the target display via an Output Transform (RRT + ODT) ([ACES Documentation](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fdocs.acescentral.com%2F); [OpenColorIO Documentation](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fopencolorio.readthedocs.io%2Fprojects%2Fconfig-aces%2Fen%2Flatest%2F)).
Why:

- Emotion: Preserves natural highlight roll-off (desaturating smoothly toward white like photochemical film) rather than harsh digital channel clipping that feels cheap and video-like.
- Attention: Keeps specular highlights, light blooms, and defocus bokeh luminous and physically compelling.
- Production value: Eliminates dark halos around alpha-composited edges, pinched clipped highlights, and incorrect motion-blur trails caused by gamma-encoded math.
- Clarity: Ensures an asset's material color looks consistent regardless of lighting exposure or target delivery display (`sRGB`, `BT.709`, `Display P3`, or `BT.2100 HDR`).
- Cost: Using a declarative [OpenColorIO (OCIO)](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fopencolorio.readthedocs.io%2Fprojects%2Fconfig-aces%2Fen%2Flatest%2F) configuration automates all color conversions across 2D, 3D, and compositing stages with zero manual per-shot gamma hacks.
  Rules:

1. Strictly separate **Scene-Referred Linear Data** (radiometric light ratios in `[0.0, +∞)`, middle gray `18%` reflectance = `0.18`, no upper clamp at `1.0`) from **Display-Referred Nonlinear Data** (transfer-function-encoded pixel values bounded to `[0.0, 1.0]` representing emitted monitor luminance) ([ACES Documentation](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fdocs.acescentral.com%2F)).
2. Configure the pipeline around the four canonical [ACES / OpenColorIO](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fopencolorio.readthedocs.io%2Fprojects%2Fconfig-aces%2Fen%2Flatest%2F) stages:
   - **Input Transform (IDT)**: Linearizes 8-bit/16-bit display-referred textures (e.g., removing the sRGB piecewise EOTF $\approx x^{2.2}$) into the working space for albedo/color maps, while marking data maps (`normal`, `roughness`, `metallic`, `displacement`, `ao`) as **`Raw / Non-Color Data`** (zero gamma transform).
   - **Working Space**: Execute all 3D shading, lightmap baking, alpha compositing, defocus blur, and motion blur in **Scene-Linear RGB**—either `Linear Rec.709 / sRGB` for standard-gamut projects or **`ACEScg` (AP1 primaries, linear transfer)** for wide-gamut studio pipelines ([ACES Documentation](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fdocs.acescentral.com%2F)).
   - **Look Modification Transform (LMT)**: Apply creative scene-referred grading in linear or log (`ACEScct`) space before tone mapping.
   - **Output Transform (RRT + ODT)**: Map unbounded scene-linear `[0.0, +∞)` through a sigmoid filmic tone mapper (Reference Rendering Transform) and encode to the specific display's gamut and inverse EOTF (Output Device Transform).
3. **Graphic / Brand Hex Color Exception**: When a brief mandates an exact display-referred brand hex code (e.g., `#FF3B30`) on a flat 2D UI or logo element, either composite that 2D layer _after_ the 3D scene's RRT/ODT tone-mapping pass OR apply an **Inverse Output Transform (Inverse ODT)** to the swatch so that after passing through the RRT/ODT it measures within $\Delta E_{00} \le 1.0$ of the brand hex code (`[OPINION]`; sources conflict between pure scene-referred purists who accept tone-mapped desaturation of bright logos and commercial broadcast specs that require exact brand hex match—**recommend**: composite flat 2D brand logos/UI in display-referred linear space post-tone-mapping, and use scene-referred shading for 3D integrated elements).
   Event mapping: Scene configuration block `color_pipeline(ocio_config="aces_v2", working_space="ACEScg"|"linear_rec709", view_transform="ACES_1.3_SDR_Video"|"AgX", display_space="Rec.709"|"sRGB"|"Display_P3")`.
   Raster/bake note: All framebuffers and baked lightmaps in the GPU rasterizer must use floating-point formats (`R16G16B16A16_SFLOAT` or `R11G11B10_UFLOAT_PACK32` when no alpha is needed) so emissive surfaces and specular highlights (`intensity = 2.0–50.0`) drive physical bloom and motion blur before the final full-screen Output Transform pass.
   Cultural notes: High-end VFX and animation studios standardize on ACES (`ACES 1.3` / `ACES 2.0` via OCIO), while stylized 3D and game-engine raster pipelines frequently use `AgX` or `Filmic` tone mappers for smoother chromatic highlight desaturation.
   Failure modes: Forgetting to mark a Normal Map or Roughness Map as `Raw / Non-Color` → the pipeline applies an sRGB-to-Linear `2.2` curve to the normal vectors, causing all surface lighting directions to tilt and crease; blurring or alpha-over compositing in gamma-encoded sRGB → dark muddy fringes around glowing objects and dim, lifeless motion blur trails.
   Checklist: [ ] Working space is scene-linear (`ACEScg` or `Linear Rec.709`) in `fp16` buffers [ ] Albedo textures linearized via IDT; Normal/Roughness/Metallic marked `Raw` [ ] Blurs, alpha-overs, and light bakes executed in linear space [ ] Tone-mapping Output Transform applied once at the very end of the pipeline
   Sources: [ACES Official Documentation](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fdocs.acescentral.com%2F); [OpenColorIO Configuration for ACES](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fopencolorio.readthedocs.io%2Fprojects%2Fconfig-aces%2Fen%2Flatest%2F); [Vulkan Specification](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fdocs.vulkan.org%2Fspec%2Flatest%2Findex.html).

### 5.2 Primary and Secondary Grading, LUTs, Tone Mapping, and Output Transforms

Definition: The mathematical manipulation of exposure, white balance, contrast, and isolated hue/luminance ranges (grading), combined with Lookup Tables (LUTs) and standard Output Transforms for SDR and HDR delivery targets.
Why:

- Emotion: Establishes the final atmospheric temperature, contrast tension, and tactile filmic cohesion across all shots.
- Attention: Uses secondary qualifiers and power windows/vignettes to subtly darken peripheral distractions by `0.3–0.8 stops` and elevate the subject.
- Production value: Proper log-space grading and tetrahedral 3D LUT interpolation prevent clipped shadows, solarized skin tones, and posterized skies.
- Clarity: Normalizes shot-to-shot brightness and color balance so cuts within a scene feel continuous.
- Cost: Executing primary/secondary grades as parametric shader uniforms and a single combined 3D LUT texture lookup runs in `< 0.05 ms` on the GPU.
  Rules:

1. Execute **Primary Grading** in a perceptually uniform Log or Scene-Linear working space before the Output Transform:
   - **Exposure (Linear Gain)** and **White Balance (Chromatic Adaptation)**: adjust in Scene-Linear space (`RGB_linear * 2^stops`).
   - **Lift / Gamma / Gain (or Offset / Power / Slope per ASC CDL)** and **S-Curve Contrast**: adjust in log working space (`ACEScct` or log intermediate working space) pivoting around middle gray (`0.18` linear $\approx 0.4135$ in `ACEScct`) ([ACES Documentation](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fdocs.acescentral.com%2F); [DaVinci Resolve Manual](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fdocuments.blackmagicdesign.com%2FUserManuals%2FDaVinci-Resolve-20-Beginners-Guide.pdf%3F_v%3D1757574013000)).
2. Constrain **Secondary Grading** (HSL/OKLCH qualifiers and spatial masks) with soft feather roll-offs (`Δh feather ≥ 15°`, `ΔL feather ≥ 0.08`, spatial mask feather `≥ 5%` of frame width); hard-edged color keys create visible tearing and chatter across animated frames.
3. Never feed unbounded scene-linear `[0.0, +∞)` values directly into a standard `[0.0, 1.0]` **3D LUT**: always sandwich a 3D LUT (`33×33×33` or `65×65×65` `.cube` with **tetrahedral interpolation**, never trilinear) inside a deterministic **Shaper Log Space** (`Linear -> Log Shaper -> 3D LUT -> Linear` or display output) so highlights above `1.0` are not hard-clipped upon entry ([OpenColorIO Documentation](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fopencolorio.readthedocs.io%2Fprojects%2Fconfig-aces%2Fen%2Flatest%2F)).
4. Match the **Output Transform** and container metadata tags to the exact delivery specification:

| Target Deliverable Standard                                                                                                        | Color Primaries                  | Transfer Function (EOTF / OETF)             | Peak Luminance / Surround                                                                                                                                                                                         | Mandatory Container NCLC / Metadata Tags                                                       |
| :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Broadcast HD & Web Video ([ITU-R BT.709](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.itu.int%2Frec%2Fr-rec-bt.709))**      | BT.709 (`D65` white)             | BT.1886 (`γ = 2.4` EOTF; `~1.96` OETF)      | `100 cd/m²` (nits), dim surround (`5 nits`)                                                                                                                                                                       | `Primaries: 1, Transfer: 1, Matrix: 1` (`1-1-1`)                                               |
| **Web Canvas / UI / Desktop ([IEC 61966-2-1 sRGB](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.w3.org%2FTR%2FWCAG22%2F))**    | BT.709 / sRGB (`D65`)            | Piecewise sRGB (`γ ≈ 2.2` effective)        | `80–160 cd/m²`, office/bright surround                                                                                                                                                                            | `Primaries: 1, Transfer: 13 (sRGB), Matrix: 1`                                                 |
| **Wide-Gamut Mobile / Cinema (Display P3)**                                                                                        | DCI-P3 primaries + `D65` white   | Piecewise sRGB (`γ ≈ 2.2`) or BT.1886       | `100–500 cd/m²`                                                                                                                                                                                                   | `Primaries: 9 (or 12 P3-D65), Transfer: 1/13, Matrix: 1`                                       |
| **UHD HDR10 / Dolby Vision ([ITU-R BT.2100 PQ](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.itu.int%2Frec%2Fr-rec-bt.2100))** | BT.2020 (`D65` white, container) | SMPTE ST 2084 **PQ** (Perceptual Quantizer) | `1,000–10,000 cd/m²` absolute; SDR diffuse white at `203 nits` ([ITU-R Report BT.2390](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.itu.int%2Fdms_pub%2Fitu-r%2Fopb%2Frep%2FR-REP-BT.2390-8-2020-PDF-E.pdf)) | `Primaries: 9 (BT.2020), Transfer: 16 (PQ), Matrix: 9` + `MaxCLL / MaxFALL` (`10-bit` minimum) |
| **Broadcast Live HDR ([ITU-R BT.2100 HLG](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.itu.int%2Frec%2Fr-rec-bt.2100))**      | BT.2020 (`D65` white)            | ARIB STD-B67 **HLG** (Hybrid Log-Gamma)     | `1,000 cd/m²` nominal, scene-referred relative                                                                                                                                                                    | `Primaries: 9, Transfer: 18 (HLG), Matrix: 9` (`10-bit` minimum)                               |

Event mapping: Post-processing shader pass `grade_and_output_pass(exposure_ev, wb_temp, cdl_slope, cdl_offset, cdl_power, saturation_oklab, lut_3d_id, output_transform="BT709_2.4")`.
Raster/bake note: Bake the combined LMT + RRT + ODT chain into a single `65×65×65` 3D LUT preceded by an analytic `ACEScct` or `AgX Log` shaper function in the final full-screen fragment shader pass for single-instruction hardware texture evaluation.
Cultural notes: The display transfer-function tag mismatch ("1-1-1 vs. 1-2-1 gamma shift") (`1-1-1` vs. `1-2-1` NCLC tag mismatch) causes washed-out blacks on color-managed OS displays when exporting `BT.709` (`γ = 2.4`) without explicit `1-1-1` or `1-2-1` tagging; for multi-platform web social delivery (`YouTube`, `Instagram`, `TikTok`), grading at `γ = 2.2` (or `γ = 2.4` in a dim room with verified `1-1-1` tagging and black levels anchored at `IRE 1–2`) prevents lifted milky shadows `[OPINION]`.
Failure modes: Applying a `17×17×17` display-referred LUT with trilinear interpolation to a high-contrast grade → severe color banding and clipped neon highlights; placing graphics at `100%` peak white (`1,000 nits`) in an HDR `BT.2100 PQ` timeline instead of `203 nits` SDR reference white → subtitles and logos sear the viewer's eyes ([ITU-R Report BT.2390](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.itu.int%2Fdms_pub%2Fitu-r%2Fopb%2Frep%2FR-REP-BT.2390-8-2020-PDF-E.pdf)).
Checklist: [ ] Exposure/WB adjusted in linear; contrast/CDL adjusted in log around `0.18` pivot [ ] 3D LUTs are `≥ 33³` with tetrahedral interpolation and log shaper [ ] Secondary qualifiers feathered `≥ 15°` hue / `≥ 0.08` lightness [ ] Output NCLC tags (`1-1-1` for SDR BT.709) embedded in final stream [ ] HDR graphics anchored at `203 nits` diffuse white (PQ)
Sources: [ITU-R BT.709](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.itu.int%2Frec%2Fr-rec-bt.709); [ITU-R BT.2020](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.itu.int%2Frec%2Fr-rec-bt.2020-2-201510-i%2Fen); [ITU-R BT.2100](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.itu.int%2Frec%2Fr-rec-bt.2100); [ITU-R Report BT.2390](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.itu.int%2Fdms_pub%2Fitu-r%2Fopb%2Frep%2FR-REP-BT.2390-8-2020-PDF-E.pdf); [ACES Documentation](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fdocs.acescentral.com%2F); [OpenColorIO Documentation](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fopencolorio.readthedocs.io%2Fprojects%2Fconfig-aces%2Fen%2Flatest%2F).

## 6. 2D Motion Graphics

### 6.1 Planar Elements, Shapes, Strokes, Curves, Masks, and Track Mattes

Definition: Parametric 2D vector primitives (rectangles, ellipses, polygons, cubic Bézier splines) and stencil/alpha masking hierarchies positioned on `+X/+Y` planar cards (`camera looks -Z`) ([Lottie v1.0 Specification](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Flottie.github.io%2Flottie-spec%2F1.0%2F)).
Why:

- Emotion: Sharp geometric polygons and mitered strokes project crisp technological precision; rounded caps, joins, and organic Bézier curves project approachable warmth ([Block, The Visual Story](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.routledge.com%2FThe-Visual-Story-Creating-the-Visual-Structure-of-Film-TV-and-Digital-Media%2FBlock%2Fp%2Fbook%2F9781138014152)).
- Attention: Animated masks and track mattes create localized windows of discovery, guiding the eye inside bounded graphic containers.
- Production value: Maintaining constant world-space stroke width during non-uniform container scaling separates professional procedural vector work from stretched bitmap scaling.
- Clarity: Clean boolean masks and alpha/luma mattes isolate overlapping graphic layers without messy overlapping contours.
- Cost: Parametric vector shapes and stencil/alpha mattes render at negligible vertex/fragment cost compared to full-frame bitmap sequences.
  Rules:

1. Decouple **Stroke Width** from non-uniform parent scale transforms (`non_scaling_stroke = true`): when a rectangular card scales from `(1.0, 1.0)` to `(2.5, 0.4)`, its border stroke must remain a uniform pixel width (`e.g., 3.0 px` or `0.28% H`) on both horizontal and vertical edges ([Lottie v1.0 Specification](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Flottie.github.io%2Flottie-spec%2F1.0%2F)).
2. Enforce **Concentric Corner Radius Math**: when nesting a rounded rectangle with inner padding $P$ inside an outer rounded container of radius $R_{\text{outer}}$, set the inner corner radius to $R_{\text{inner}} = \max(0,\; R_{\text{outer}} - P)$ so the Negative Space gap maintains uniform radial thickness around the corner arc `[OPINION]`.
3. Choose **Masking vs. Track Matte** architecture explicitly:
   - **Element-Local Mask**: Bézier path bound to the element's local transform coordinate space (moves with the element).
   - **Independent Track Matte (Alpha or Luma Matte)**: Stationary or independently animated stencil planar element in parent/world space through which the target element passes (`alpha_matte` uses the stencil's coverage channel $A$; `luma_matte` uses the stencil's BT.709 luminance $Y = 0.2126R + 0.7152G + 0.0722B$) ([Adobe After Effects Help](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fhelpx.adobe.com%2Fafter-effects%2Fdesktop.html); [Lottie v1.0 Specification](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Flottie.github.io%2Flottie-spec%2F1.0%2F)).
4. Set miter limits on sharp-angled strokes to `miter_limit = 4.0` (automatically converting angles `< 29°` to bevel joins) or use `round` joins/caps to prevent infinitely spiking stroke vertices during acute path deformations ([Lottie v1.0 Specification](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Flottie.github.io%2Flottie-spec%2F1.0%2F)).
   Event mapping: Declarative `planar_shape_element(geometry="bezier_spline"|"rounded_rect", stroke_width_px, cap="round"|"butt", join="round"|"miter", matte_source_id, matte_mode="alpha"|"luma")` driven by transform and path-parameter events.
   Raster/bake note: Render 2D vector splines on the GPU using either hardware MSAA (`4×–8×`) on loop-Blinn / stencil-then-cover triangulated meshes or analytic Signed Distance Field (SDF) fragment shaders (`smoothstep(-0.7px, +0.7px, sdf_dist)`) for sub-pixel anti-aliasing.
   Cultural notes: Flat vector motion graphics ("corporate Memphis" vs. geometric Swiss motion) dominate SaaS and explainer media; combining textured luma mattes (ink bleeds, halftone screens, paper grain) with crisp vector geometry bridges digital precision and tactile analog craft `[OPINION]`.
   Failure modes: Scaling a stroked rectangle non-uniformly without `non_scaling_stroke` → vertical borders become thick bars while horizontal borders vanish; setting $R_{\text{inner}} = R_{\text{outer}}$ on nested cards → corners look pinched and non-parallel.
   Checklist: [ ] `non_scaling_stroke` enabled on non-uniformly scaled shapes [ ] Nested corner radii follow $R_{\text{inner}} = \max(0, R_{\text{outer}} - P)$ [ ] Miter limit capped at `4.0` or round joins used [ ] Track mattes used for world-space reveals; local masks for element-locked crops
   Sources: [Lottie v1.0 Specification](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Flottie.github.io%2Flottie-spec%2F1.0%2F); [Block, The Visual Story](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.routledge.com%2FThe-Visual-Story-Creating-the-Visual-Structure-of-Film-TV-and-Digital-Media%2FBlock%2Fp%2Fbook%2F9781138014152); [Adobe After Effects Help](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fhelpx.adobe.com%2Fafter-effects%2Fdesktop.html).

### 6.2 Path Animation, Trim Operations, and Topological Morphing

Definition: The continuous parametric evaluation of stroke arc-length intervals (`trim_start`, `trim_end`, `trim_offset`), motion along a spline (`orient_along_path`), and vertex-to-vertex interpolation between two closed or open Bézier topologies (morphing) ([Lottie v1.0 Specification](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Flottie.github.io%2Flottie-spec%2F1.0%2F)).
Why:

- Emotion: Fluid trim-path tracers and seamless topological morphs create a sense of magic, continuity, and effortless kinetic flow.
- Attention: The leading tip of an animated trim path acts as a moving laser pointer that pulls the viewer's eye directly to the next focal node.
- Production value: Arc-length-parameterized speed and matched vertex winding prevent the pulsing velocity and inside-out self-intersecting twists of amateur path morphs.
- Clarity: Connects cause-and-effect nodes across diagrams, maps, and icon state changes without cutting.
- Cost: Analytic Bézier vertex interpolation on planar splines achieves fluid shape transformation at `< 1%` of the cost of 3D mesh booleans.
  Rules:

1. Reparameterize all motion paths and trim strokes by **Normalized Arc Length ($s \in [0.0, 1.0]$)** rather than raw Bézier parameter $t \in [0.0, 1.0]$ (using a `64–128` entry arc-length lookup table per cubic segment) so an element traveling at constant event speed does not suddenly speed up on long segments and stall at clustered control points `[OPINION]`.
2. For **Traveling Line / Tracer Effects** (`trim_start` chasing `trim_end`), stagger the `trim_end` event (`start = t₀`, `ease = cubic_in_out`, `duration = 16–30 frames`) ahead of the `trim_start` event (`start = t₀ + 4–10 frames`, `ease = cubic_in_out`, `duration = 16–30 frames`) so the line stretches out at peak velocity in the middle of the path and contracts smoothly to `0%` length at the destination ([Williams, The Animator's Survival Kit](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FThe_Animator%2527s_Survival_Kit); [Lottie v1.0 Specification](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Flottie.github.io%2Flottie-spec%2F1.0%2F)).
3. Enforce the **Three Laws of Topological Morphing** between Shape A and Shape B:
   - **Equal Vertex Count ($N_A = N_B$)**: Subdivide whichever spline has fewer cubic Bézier knots using De Casteljau's algorithm at matching normalized arc-length ratios before interpolating.
   - **Matched First Vertex (`index = 0`)**: Align the starting knot `v₀` of both shapes to the same topological compass quadrant (e.g., top-most vertex at `12 o'clock`) so vertices travel the shortest straight path rather than rotating around the perimeter.
   - **Matched Winding Order**: Both splines must wind in the exact same direction (`clockwise` → `clockwise` or `counter-clockwise` → `counter-clockwise`) ([Lottie v1.0 Specification](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Flottie.github.io%2Flottie-spec%2F1.0%2F)).
4. Add a `10%–20%` **Anticipation / Overshoot Scale or Squash-and-Stretch** event across the midpoint (`t = 0.5`) of a 2D shape morph (`duration = 10–18 frames`) so the intermediate state reads as an energetic physical transformation rather than a stiff linear vertex crossfade ([Thomas \& Johnston, The Illusion of Life](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.penguinrandomhouse.com%2Fbooks%2F727679%2Fillusion-of-life-the-by-frank-thomas%2F)).
   Event mapping:

- Trim: `trim_event(target_curve_id, channel="trim_end", start=t0, duration=20f, from=0.0, to=1.0, easing="cubic_in_out")` + `trim_event(target_curve_id, channel="trim_start", start=t0+6f, duration=20f, from=0.0, to=1.0, easing="cubic_in_out")`.
- Morph: `morph_event(target_spline_id, from_topology="shape_a", to_topology="shape_b", start=t0, duration=14f, easing="expo_in_out")`.
  Raster/bake note: Pre-normalize spline topologies (`N` vertices, aligned `v₀`, uniform winding) during scene compilation/baking so runtime vertex shaders only execute a fast linear/eased `mix(pos_A, pos_B, u_morph)` per vertex.
  Cultural notes: Liquid-motion 2D frame-by-frame and spline morphing (popularized by European and North American boutique motion studios) uses trailing secondary smear shapes (`2–4 frame` delayed echo paths) to bridge distant screen coordinates `[OPINION]`.
  Failure modes: Morphing two closed paths with opposite winding orders or mismatched `v₀` indices → the shape collapses through its own center into an hourglass self-intersection at `t = 0.5`; animating `trim_start` and `trim_end` with identical start times and durations → rigid worm of fixed length sliding mechanically along the path.
  Checklist: [ ] Motion/trim paths parameterized by normalized arc length $s$ [ ] Trim-start lags trim-end by `4–10 frames` for organic stretch/contract [ ] Morphing shapes share equal vertex count, aligned `v₀`, and identical winding [ ] Midpoint morph softened with anticipation/overshoot scale event
  Sources: [Lottie v1.0 Specification](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Flottie.github.io%2Flottie-spec%2F1.0%2F); [Thomas \& Johnston, The Illusion of Life](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.penguinrandomhouse.com%2Fbooks%2F727679%2Fillusion-of-life-the-by-frank-thomas%2F); [Williams, The Animator's Survival Kit](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FThe_Animator%2527s_Survival_Kit).

### 6.3 2D Transitions (Cut, Dissolve, Wipe, Push, Slide, Zoom, Morph, Mask Reveal, Luma, Whip)

Definition: The spatial, optical, or geometric handoff mechanism bridging Outgoing Scene A and Incoming Scene B across a shared temporal window (`0` to `72 frames`).
Why:

- Emotion: Dictates the perceived weight of a scene boundary—from invisible narrative continuity (Cut) or wistful memory (Dissolve) to kinetic propulsion (Whip/Zoom/Mask Reveal).
- Attention: Motion-driven transitions carry the viewer's gaze vector directly across the boundary into the incoming focal anchor.
- Production value: Motivated, speed-matched transitions with continuous velocity across the midpoint read as seamless cinematography, whereas unmotivated stock wipes read as amateur slideshow templates.
- Clarity: Signals to the viewer whether time, location, or conceptual hierarchy has changed.
- Cost: Shader-based luma mattes, pushes, and whip-pans execute in real time at zero extra scene geometry cost.
  Rules:

1. Follow the **2D Transition Parameter & Motivation Specification**:

| Transition Type               | Duration Range (@ `24–30 fps`) | Easing / Velocity Profile                                                                   | Mandatory Motivation & Execution Rule                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| :---------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Hard Cut**                  | `0 frames`                     | Instantaneous step                                                                          | Default for `≥ 85%` of all transitions; match action or eye-trace (`Δpos ≤ 15% W`) ([Murch](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FIn_the_Blink_of_an_Eye_%28Murch_book%29)).                |
| **Dissolve**                  | `24–72 frames` (`1.0–3.0 s`)   | `sine_in_out` or `linear` in **Linear Light**                                               | Signals passage of time or thematic overlap; never use `6–12 frame` "soft cuts" solely to hide a bad edit `[OPINION]`.                                                                                                        |
| **Wipe (Geometric)**          | `12–24 frames` (`0.5–1.0 s`)   | `cubic_in_out` with `2–4 frame` multi-layer stagger                                         | Edge must be angled (`15°–75°`) or multi-layered (2–3 offset color bands staggered by `2–3 frames`); avoid raw unfeathered single-line vertical wipes `[OPINION]`.                                                            |
| **Push / Slide**              | `10–20 frames` (`0.4–0.8 s`)   | `expo_in_out` or `cubic_in_out`                                                             | Incoming scene pushes outgoing scene (Push) or slides over a `20%–40%` parallax-retarded outgoing scene (Slide); align direction with subject reading vector.                                                                 |
| **Zoom (Scale-Through)**      | `10–18 frames` (`0.4–0.75 s`)  | Exponential scale $S(t) = S_0 \cdot e^{kt}$ (`expo_in` → cut at peak velocity → `expo_out`) | Scale must be **logarithmic/exponential** (`e.g., 1.0× → 8.0×` in Shot A matched to `0.125× → 1.0×` in Shot B at the midpoint cut) with radial motion blur; linear scale creates a visible speed kink at the cut `[OPINION]`. |
| **Morph Transition**          | `12–24 frames` (`0.5–1.0 s`)   | `cubic_in_out`                                                                              | Hero silhouette of Shot A transforms topologically into hero silhouette of Shot B while backgrounds cross-dissolve or wipe.                                                                                                   |
| **Mask Reveal (Object Wipe)** | `12–30 frames` (`0.5–1.25 s`)  | Matched to foreground occluder velocity                                                     | A foreground element (pillar, character back, graphic card) traverses `100%` of the frame width; clip Shot B to the trailing edge of the occluder.                                                                            |
| **Luma Matte (Gradient/Ink)** | `18–36 frames` (`0.75–1.5 s`)  | `cubic_in_out` on threshold $T \in [-f, 1+f]$                                               | Drive alpha via `smoothstep(T - feather, T + feather, luma_map)` with `feather ∈ [0.05, 0.20]` so high-luminance (or low-luminance) areas transition organically first.                                                       |
| **Whip (Swish Pan/Tilt)**     | `6–12 frames` (`0.25–0.5 s`)   | `cubic_in` (Shot A) → cut at max velocity (`≥ 25% W/frame`) → `cubic_out` (Shot B)          | Apply directional vector blur (`shutter_angle = 180°–360°`) + mirrored edge tile/wrap at the midpoint frame so seam is completely invisible.                                                                                  |

2. Enforce **Midpoint Velocity Continuity ($C^1$ Velocity Match)** on all Push, Slide, Zoom, and Whip transitions: the instantaneous velocity at the final frame of Outgoing Shot A must equal the instantaneous velocity at the first frame of Incoming Shot B (`v_out(t_cut) == v_in(t_cut)`). Stopping at the midpoint (`ease_in_out` on Shot A followed by `ease_in_out` on Shot B) creates a double-clutch stutter.
   Event mapping: Dual-scene transition event `scene_transition_event(from_scene_id, to_scene_id, type="whip"|"zoom"|"luma"|"mask_reveal", start_frame, duration_frames, direction_vec, easing="expo_in_out")` that schedules outgoing and incoming camera/compositor transforms across the overlap window.
   Raster/bake note: During transition overlap windows (`duration_frames`), render both Scene A and Scene B into off-screen `fp16` color targets (or evaluate Scene B in a single-pass stencil/luma composite when geometry allows) and execute directional/radial motion blur + linear-light blending in the composite shader.
   Cultural notes: Kurosawa and classic adventure cinema (_Star Wars_) use deliberate directional Wipes as narrative chapter markers; Edgar Wright-style kinetic editing uses `6–10 frame` Whip Pans and Object Mask Reveals to compress mundane transitions into rhythmic comedy beats `[OPINION]`.
   Failure modes: Using linear scale (`scale = mix(1.0, 10.0, t)`) on a Zoom transition → the zoom appears to rush violently at the beginning and crawl at the end because perceived optical magnification is proportional to $\Delta S / S$; chaining a Zoom transition into a Whip transition into a Spin transition within `5 seconds` → viewer motion sickness and amateur "preset pack" fatigue.
   Checklist: [ ] Hard Cuts account for `≥ 85%` of edits [ ] Dissolves blended in linear light (`≥ 24 frames`) [ ] Zoom transitions use exponential scale $S(t) = S_0 e^{kt}$ [ ] Whip/Push/Zoom velocity is continuous ($C^1$) across the midpoint cut [ ] Zero consecutive unmotivated stylized transitions
   Sources: [Murch, In the Blink of an Eye](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FIn_the_Blink_of_an_Eye_%28Murch_book%29); [Mascelli, The Five C's of Cinematography](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.silmanjamespress.com%2Fshop%2Ffilmmaking-directing%2Fthe-five-cs-of-cinematography%2F); [Lottie v1.0 Specification](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Flottie.github.io%2Flottie-spec%2F1.0%2F).

## 7. Animation Fundamentals

### 7.1 The 12 Principles in Declarative Event Systems

Definition: Frank Thomas and Ollie Johnston's 12 foundational principles of animation ([The Illusion of Life](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.penguinrandomhouse.com%2Fbooks%2F727679%2Fillusion-of-life-the-by-frank-thomas%2F)) translated from hand-drawn keyframes into explicit, numeric, declarative event-channel rules.
Why:

- Emotion: Imbues geometric primitives and meshes with perceived mass, intention, vulnerability, and vitality.
- Attention: Anticipation cues prepare the viewer's eye for where motion is about to occur; Staging guarantees the action is never obscured.
- Production value: The presence of volume-preserving squash-and-stretch, anticipation, and follow-through immediately distinguishes crafted animation from mechanical linear interpolation.
- Clarity: Exaggeration and Solid Drawing/Posing make silhouettes and emotional states unmistakable at a glance.
- Cost: Declarative event templates (automatically pre-pending an anticipation event and appending an overshoot/settle event to any primary translation cue) deliver hand-crafted quality programmatically.
  Rules:

1. Translate each of the **12 Principles** into explicit numeric event parameters:

| Principle ([Thomas \& Johnston](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.penguinrandomhouse.com%2Fbooks%2F727679%2Fillusion-of-life-the-by-frank-thomas%2F)) | Declarative Event-Driven Rule & Numeric Range                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **1. Squash and Stretch**                                                                                                                                             | Enforce constant volume/area: in 3D, $S_x \cdot S_y \cdot S_z = 1.0$ (if primary motion axis stretches to $S_y = 1.25$, set $S_x = S_z = 1/\sqrt{1.25} = 0.894$); in 2D, $S_x \cdot S_y = 1.0$. Realistic rigid/semi-rigid range: `±3%–8%`; stylized motion graphics: `±12%–25%`; slapstick cartoon: `±35%–60%`.                                                                                                         |
| **2. Anticipation**                                                                                                                                                   | Pre-pend a counter-directional event before any major primary motion: displacement = `8%–18%` of primary travel distance in the opposite direction (`-v_dir`), duration = `20%–35%` of primary action duration (`4–10 frames` @ `24 fps`), with a `1–2 frame` apex hold before the forward launch ([Williams](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FThe_Animator%2527s_Survival_Kit)). |
| **3. Staging**                                                                                                                                                        | Only `1` primary kinetic event may peak within any `6–8 frame` (`250 ms`) window; maintain silhouette separation (`≥ 2% W` negative space gap around active limb/element) and luminance contrast (`ΔL ≥ 0.35`).                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| **4. Straight Ahead vs. Pose-to-Pose**                                                                                                                                | Model **Pose-to-Pose** as discrete declarative state cues (`pose_event` at explicit timestamps `t₀, t₁, t₂`); model **Straight Ahead** as procedural/additive channel modifiers (noise, spring physics, or baked particle/fluid caches) layered on top of the pose sequence.                                                                                                                                             |
| **5. Follow-Through & Overlapping Action**                                                                                                                            | Append child/extremity phase lags (`Δt = 2–6 frames` per hierarchy depth level) and damped settle oscillations (`1–3 cycles`, amplitude `3%–12%` of travel) after the root element stops.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| **6. Slow In and Slow Out**                                                                                                                                           | Ensure `≥ 90%` of non-continuous transform events use nonlinear easing (`cubic`, `expo`, `spring`), spending `60%–75%` of frames near the start/end poses and only `25%–40%` of frames in the high-velocity middle transit.                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| **7. Arcs**                                                                                                                                                           | Whenever an element translates diagonally across `(ΔX, ΔY)`, inject an orthogonal bow offset (`arc_bulge = 8%–20%` of chord length) or decouple X and Y easing curves (e.g., `X = cubic_out`, `Y = quad_in_out`) so the trajectory forms a smooth parabola rather than a rigid straight line.                                                                                                                            |
| **8. Secondary Action**                                                                                                                                               | Attach `1–2` subordinate supporting events (e.g., micro-tilt `3°–8°`, surface sheen pulse, or small companion particle puff) triggered at `t_primary + 2f` with screen area `≤ 20%` of the primary action; never let secondary action overpower the primary read.                                                                                                                                                        |
| **9. Timing**                                                                                                                                                         | Map frame counts @ `24 fps` to physical mass and intent: `2–4 frames` = instantaneous snap/shock; `6–10 frames` = crisp, light, energetic UI/graphic action; `14–24 frames` = standard natural weight; `30–60+ frames` = massive scale, heavy inertia, or solemn restraint ([Williams](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FThe_Animator%2527s_Survival_Kit)).                        |
| **10. Exaggeration**                                                                                                                                                  | Push peak pose extremes (`scale`, `tilt`, `chroma`) by `+15%–35%` beyond physically literal values for `2–4 frames` around the motion apex so the kinetic energy survives motion blur and viewer blink intervals.                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| **11. Solid Drawing (3D Form)**                                                                                                                                       | Avoid parallel twin axes ("twinning"): offset left/right paired elements or limbs by `≥ 10%–20%` in angle and `2–4 frames` in timing; orient 3D meshes at `3/4` view angles (`25°–65°` yaw, `10°–25°` pitch) so three orthogonal planes remain visible.                                                                                                                                                                  |
| **12. Appeal**                                                                                                                                                        | Enforce asymmetric proportion ratios (`1 : 1.618` or `60 : 30 : 10`) across character/graphic segments and pair complex detailed areas with resting smooth areas (detail-to-rest ratio `≤ 30 : 70`).                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |

Event mapping: Composite macro-event `kinetic_move_event(target_id, from_pos, to_pos, start=t0, duration=16f)` that compiles into a 3-stage sequence on the element's channels: (1) `anticipation_cue(t0 - 5f, dur=5f, pos=from_pos - 0.12*delta, scale=[1.06, 0.94])`, (2) `transit_cue(t0, dur=16f, pos=to_pos, arc_offset=0.15, scale_peak=[0.88, 1.14])`, and (3) `settle_cue(t0 + 16f, dur=8f, spring_zeta=0.72)`.
Raster/bake note: Evaluate volume-preserving squash-and-stretch (`S_x * S_y * S_z = 1.0`) along the velocity tangent vector inside the vertex shader or transform hierarchy matrix (`R_vel * S_squash * R_vel^T`) so meshes deform along their direction of travel regardless of local pivot orientation.
Cultural notes: Western feature animation (classical feature animation) emphasizes continuous fluid arcs, overlap, and squash-and-stretch on `1s` (`24 fps`); Japanese _Limited Animation_ (stylized limited animation) intentionally holds extreme key poses for `6–18 frames` and transitions between them in `1–3 frames` with hand-drawn/mesh smear frames for explosive high-contrast impact `[OPINION]`.
Failure modes: Stretching an axis to `1.4×` without compressing the perpendicular axes to `0.714×` (2D) or `0.845×` (3D) → the object appears to inflate and grow in mass mid-flight; omitting anticipation on a fast `6-frame` launch → the viewer's eye only notices the object _after_ it has already arrived at its destination.
Checklist: [ ] Squash-and-stretch preserves volume ($S_x S_y S_z = 1.0$) [ ] Major launches preceded by `4–10 frame` (`8%–18%` distance) anticipation [ ] Only `1` primary action peaks per `6–8 frame` window [ ] Diagonal translations travel on curved arcs (`8%–20%` bow) [ ] Zero symmetrical "twinning" in paired limbs/elements
Sources: [Thomas \& Johnston, The Illusion of Life](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.penguinrandomhouse.com%2Fbooks%2F727679%2Fillusion-of-life-the-by-frank-thomas%2F); [Williams, The Animator's Survival Kit](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FThe_Animator%2527s_Survival_Kit).

### 7.2 Timing vs. Spacing and Easing Families (Linear, Quad, Cubic, Expo, Back, Elastic, Bounce, Spring)

Definition: The separation of **Timing** (the exact frame timestamps `start` and `duration` when key events occur) from **Spacing** (how closely or widely intermediate positions are clustered between those timestamps via mathematical easing or spring functions) ([Williams, The Animator's Survival Kit](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FThe_Animator%2527s_Survival_Kit)).
Why:

- Emotion: Spacing defines the perceived personality and material physics of an element—from crisp, decisive intelligence (`expo-out`) to heavy industrial machinery (`cubic-in-out`) or playful buoyancy (`spring` / `elastic`).
- Attention: High initial acceleration (`ease-out`) creates an immediate velocity spike at `t = start`, capturing peripheral vision on frame 1 of the event.
- Production value: Using default symmetrical `ease-in-out` (`cubic-bezier(0.42, 0, 0.58, 1)`) on every single channel produces a floaty, robotic "underwater" feel that marks amateur work.
- Clarity: Rapid deceleration into a long settle ensures the element is nearly stationary and readable for the final `40%` of its event duration.
- Cost: Analytic easing curves and closed-form damped harmonic oscillators evaluate in nanoseconds per channel with zero baked curve tables.
  Rules:

1. Apply the **Canonical Easing & Spring Parameter Specification**:

| Easing Family            | Cubic-Bézier `(x1, y1, x2, y2)` or Physics Formula                                                               | Optimal Duration (`ms` / `frames @ 24 fps`) | Primary Use Case & Felt Effect                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| :----------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Linear**               | `(0.00, 0.00, 1.00, 1.00)`                                                                                       | Any continuous window                       | Constant-rate mechanical rotation, UV scrolling, slow documentary camera creep, or linear-light cross-dissolves.                                                                                                                                     |
| **Quadratic (`Quad`)**   | Out: `(0.25, 0.46, 0.45, 0.94)`<br>In-Out: `(0.45, 0.03, 0.51, 0.95)`                                            | `150–300 ms` (`4–7 f`)                      | Gentle, low-energy micro-transitions (opacity fades, subtle color shifts, minor hover/highlight states).                                                                                                                                             |
| **Cubic**                | Out: `(0.22, 1.00, 0.36, 1.00)`<br>In: `(0.32, 0.00, 0.67, 0.00)`<br>In-Out: `(0.65, 0.00, 0.35, 1.00)`          | `250–500 ms` (`6–12 f`)                     | Workhorse standard for natural typography reveals, medium camera moves, and clean graphic layout transitions.                                                                                                                                        |
| **Exponential (`Expo`)** | Out: `(0.16, 1.00, 0.30, 1.00)`<br>In-Out: `(0.87, 0.00, 0.13, 1.00)`                                            | `400–800 ms` (`10–20 f`)                    | High-craft editorial snap: explosive initial velocity that spends the final `60%` of duration asymptotically settling into pixel-perfect alignment `[OPINION]`.                                                                                      |
| **Back (Overshoot)**     | Out: `(0.34, 1.56, 0.64, 1.00)`<br>(Overshoot param $s \in [1.1, 1.7]$)                                          | `300–550 ms` (`7–13 f`)                     | Single clean overshoot (`4%–10%` past target) for tactile card pop-ins, badge reveals, and energetic limb locks.                                                                                                                                     |
| **Damped Spring**        | $x(t) = 1 - e^{-\zeta \omega_n t}\left(\cos(\omega_d t) + \frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}}\sin(\omega_d t)\right)$ | Settle within `350–750 ms` (`8–18 f`)       | Physically grounded mass: use **Critically Damped** ($\zeta = 1.0$) for zero-overshoot luxury UI, **Slightly Underdamped** ($\zeta \in [0.68, 0.85]$, $\omega_n \in [14, 24]\text{ rad/s}$) for natural physical settle (`1–2` subtle oscillations). |
| **Elastic**              | Underdamped Spring with $\zeta \in [0.20, 0.45]$, $\omega_n \in [25, 45]\text{ rad/s}$                           | `500–900 ms` (`12–22 f`)                    | High-tension rubber band, string pluck, or cartoon boing (`3–5` visible oscillations); use sparingly (`≤ 1` element per scene) `[OPINION]`.                                                                                                          |
| **Bounce**               | Piecewise parabolic gravity arcs with coefficient of restitution $e \in [0.45, 0.68]$                            | `450–850 ms` (`11–20 f`)                    | Rigid collision against an unyielding floor/wall; each bounce duration scales by $e$ and height scales by $e^2$.                                                                                                                                     |

2. Enforce **Asymmetric In-Out Bias** on point-to-point moves: when an element moves from A to B inside the frame, make the acceleration phase faster (`30%–35%` of total time) and the deceleration phase longer (`65%–70%` of total time, e.g., `cubic-bezier(0.4, 0.0, 0.15, 1.0)`) so the motion feels propelled by an initial impulse rather than symmetrically drifting `[OPINION]`.
3. Never apply `Back`, `Elastic`, or `Bounce` easing to **Opacity** or **Lightness** channels—overshooting opacity above `1.0` or below `0.0` either clamps invisibly (wasting frames) or causes an unintended flash.
   Event mapping: Every `event` carries an explicit `easing` descriptor: either `easing: {type: "cubic_bezier", p: [0.16, 1.0, 0.3, 1.0]}` or `easing: {type: "spring", zeta: 0.75, omega_n: 18.0, v0: 0.0}`.
   Raster/bake note: Evaluate `cubic_bezier` via 4-iteration Newton-Raphson root-finding on $x(t)$ and evaluate `spring` via the exact analytical closed-form damped harmonic equation in the event runtime—never use Euler integration with variable frame steps, which causes spring amplitude to diverge across `24 fps` vs. `60 fps` renders.
   Cultural notes: High-fashion and architectural motion design relies almost exclusively on long, frictionless `Expo-Out` (`600–1000 ms`) and critically damped springs ($\zeta = 1.0$); mobile game and kids' broadcast motion relies heavily on `Back-Out` ($s = 1.5$) and `Elastic` ($\zeta = 0.35$) `[OPINION]`.
   Failure modes: Applying `Linear` easing to a camera pan or object slide → the start and stop frames look like the object hit an invisible brick wall; using `Expo-In-Out` with a short `6-frame` duration → the middle transit jumps `80%` of the screen width in a single frame, breaking visual tracking.
   Checklist: [ ] Entrances use `Ease-Out`; exits use `Ease-In`; in-frame moves use asymmetric `In-Out` or `Spring` [ ] Spring physics evaluated analytically ($\zeta \in [0.68, 1.0]$ for standard work) [ ] Zero `Back`/`Elastic`/`Bounce` curves applied to opacity channels [ ] `Expo` curves given sufficient duration (`≥ 10 frames` @ `24 fps`) so mid-transit does not teleport
   Sources: [Williams, The Animator's Survival Kit](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FThe_Animator%2527s_Survival_Kit); [Lottie v1.0 Specification](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Flottie.github.io%2Flottie-spec%2F1.0%2F); [Khronos glTF 2.0 Animation Spec](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fgithub.com%2FKhronosGroup%2FglTF%2Fblob%2Fmain%2Fspecification%2F2.0%2FSpecification.adoc).

### 7.3 Overlap, Follow-Through, Secondary Motion, Holds, Arcs, and Stagger/Offset

Definition: The temporal phase-shifting of channels across a single hierarchy (overlap/follow-through) or across an array of sibling elements (stagger/offset), combined with deliberate stationary holds and curved spatial trajectories ([Thomas \& Johnston, The Illusion of Life](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.penguinrandomhouse.com%2Fbooks%2F727679%2Fillusion-of-life-the-by-frank-thomas%2F); [Williams, The Animator's Survival Kit](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FThe_Animator%2527s_Survival_Kit)).
Why:

- Emotion: Breaks up rigid mechanical synchronicity so multi-part objects and ensembles feel alive, fluid, and choreographed.
- Attention: Staggered cascades guide the viewer's eye sequentially across a group rather than presenting a simultaneous wall of motion.
- Production value: Offsetting rotation by `2–4 frames` behind translation ("breaking joints") is one of the highest-leverage craft secrets in both 2D and 3D animation ([Williams, The Animator's Survival Kit](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FThe_Animator%2527s_Survival_Kit)).
- Clarity: Deliberate holds (`6–18 frames`) after high-velocity moves give the viewer's visual cortex time to register the new state.
- Cost: Algorithmic stagger functions (`start_i = t0 + i * Δt`) and hierarchical delay offsets generate rich complexity from a single base event definition.
  Rules:

1. **Channel Phase Offset (Single Element Overlap)**: Never start and end `Translation`, `Rotation`, and `Scale` on the exact same frame for an organic or expressive element. Offset `Rotation` by `+1 to +3 frames` after `Translation` starts, and allow `Rotation` settle to trail `+2 to +5 frames` after `Translation` finishes ([Williams, The Animator's Survival Kit](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FThe_Animator%2527s_Survival_Kit)).
2. **Hierarchical Chain Delay (Wave / Whip Principle)**: In a parent-to-child chain of $K$ segments (e.g., spine → shoulder → elbow → wrist, or card → header → icon → label), delay each child level $k \in \{1 \dots K\}$ by $\Delta t = 2\text{–}4\text{ frames}$ (`80–165 ms`) relative to its immediate parent, with angular amplitude decaying or amplifying by `0.75×–1.25×` per link depending on material stiffness ([Williams, The Animator's Survival Kit](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FThe_Animator%2527s_Survival_Kit)).
3. **Ensemble Stagger Budgeting**: When staggering an array of $N$ sibling elements (e.g., grid cards, list items, kinetic words), cap the **Total Cascade Spread** ($(N - 1) \times \Delta t_{\text{stagger}}$) at `≤ 350–600 ms` (`8–15 frames` @ `24 fps`) regardless of how large $N$ is:
   $$\Delta t_{\text{stagger}} = \min\left(3\text{ frames},\; \frac{14\text{ frames}}{\max(1, N - 1)}\right)$$
   Apply an **Eased Stagger Distribution** (`quad_out` on the index offset $i/(N-1)$) so the first 2–3 items establish a clear rhythm and the remaining items cascade rapidly without making the viewer wait `3 seconds` for a 20-item grid to finish appearing `[OPINION]`.
4. **Moving Holds vs. Dead Holds**: In 3D and rich 2D scenes, replace "dead holds" (0.0 velocity across all channels, which makes the render look like a frozen still frame) with a **Moving Hold**—continue a micro-drift along the primary easing vector at `1%–3%` of peak velocity or apply a subtle breathing scale/drift (`±0.3%` scale over `48 frames`) during the reading window ([Thomas \& Johnston, The Illusion of Life](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.penguinrandomhouse.com%2Fbooks%2F727679%2Fillusion-of-life-the-by-frank-thomas%2F)). (Exception: Japanese anime / spider-verse stylized step-timing intentionally uses `0.0` velocity dead holds on `2s`/`3s` for graphic contrast).
   Event mapping:

- Hierarchy overlap: `channel_offset_rule(target_id, translate_delay=0f, rotate_delay=+2f, scale_delay=+1f, child_chain_lag=+3f)`.
- Ensemble stagger: `group_stagger_event(target_group_ids[], base_event, total_spread_max_frames=14, index_distribution="quad_out"|"radial_from_center")`.
  Raster/bake note: When rendering large staggered ensembles (`N > 100` instances), pass each instance's computed `start_time_offset` as a per-instance vertex attribute (`vec4 instance_timing`) and evaluate the easing curve in the vertex shader via hardware instancing (`vkCmdDrawIndexedInstanced`).
  Cultural notes: Classical Disney feature animation mandates Moving Holds to preserve the illusion of dimensionality; graphical motion design (contemporary design-driven motion studios) combines ultra-crisp `Expo-Out` primary motion with `2–3 frame` hierarchical secondary overlap on every sub-component `[OPINION]`.
  Failure modes: Using a fixed `4-frame` stagger on a 25-item grid → the cascade drags on for `100 frames` (`> 4 seconds`), boring the viewer to tears; stopping a 3D character or product mesh at `0.0` velocity across all axes for `3 seconds` → the object looks like a paused video screenshot ("dead hold").
  Checklist: [ ] Rotation/scale channels offset by `1–3 frames` from translation [ ] Child hierarchy links lag parents by `2–4 frames` [ ] Total ensemble stagger spread capped at `≤ 14–15 frames` (`≤ 600 ms`) [ ] 3D holds use `1%–3%` micro-drift Moving Holds (unless stylized stepped timing)
  Sources: [Thomas \& Johnston, The Illusion of Life](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.penguinrandomhouse.com%2Fbooks%2F727679%2Fillusion-of-life-the-by-frank-thomas%2F); [Williams, The Animator's Survival Kit](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fen.wikipedia.org%2Fwiki%2FThe_Animator%2527s_Survival_Kit).

## 8. 3D Composition

### 8.1 Spatial Layout, Staging, and Depth Cues (Overlap, Scale, Atmosphere, DoF, Parallax)

Definition: The organization of meshes and planar elements across Foreground (FG), Midground (MG), and Background (BG) Z-depth planes using five monocular depth cues to create compelling 3D dimensionality on a flat 2D projection ([Block, The Visual Story](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.routledge.com%2FThe-Visual-Story-Creating-the-Visual-Structure-of-Film-TV-and-Digital-Media%2FBlock%2Fp%2Fbook%2F9781138014152)).
Why:

- Emotion: Deep-space staging with strong perspective convergence and parallax induces awe, scale, and immersion; flat-space orthographic staging induces detachment, graphic playfulness, or claustrophobia ([Block, The Visual Story](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.routledge.com%2FThe-Visual-Story-Creating-the-Visual-Structure-of-Film-TV-and-Digital-Media%2FBlock%2Fp%2Fbook%2F9781138014152)).
- Attention: Combining sharp mid-ground focus with foreground/background depth-of-field blur and atmospheric haze funnels gaze directly onto the hero Z-plane.
- Production value: Layering at least three distinct Z-planes with foreground framing elements ("dirtying the frame") immediately elevates a 3D scene from a sterile asset viewer to cinema.
- Clarity: Clear silhouette overlap and atmospheric contrast compression prevent background clutter from merging with the foreground subject's edges.
- Cost: Using low-poly meshes or camera-facing planar cards in the far background with analytical exponential fog achieves vast perceived world scale at minimal vertex cost.
  Rules:

1. Divide every 3D scene along the camera's `-Z` view axis into three explicit depth zones with logarithmic distance ratios:
   - **Foreground (FG / "Dirty Frame" Layer)**: $Z_{\text{FG}} \in [0.25 \cdot Z_{\text{MG}},\; 0.60 \cdot Z_{\text{MG}}]$, occupying `10%–25%` of peripheral frame area (e.g., out-of-focus foliage, architectural pillar, floating particles) darkened by `-1.0 to -2.0 EV` relative to the midground to frame the subject ([Brown, Cinematography: Theory and Practice](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.routledge.com%2FCinematography-Theory-and-Practice-For-Cinematographers-and-Directors%2FBrown%2Fp%2Fbook%2F9780367373450)).
   - **Midground (MG / Hero Action Plane)**: $Z_{\text{MG}} = 1.0 \times$ focal distance, holding peak luminance contrast ($\Delta L \ge 0.45$), peak chroma, and zero defocus blur (`Circle of Confusion ≤ 1.0 px`).
   - **Background (BG / Environment Layer)**: $Z_{\text{BG}} \ge 2.5 \cdot Z_{\text{MG}}$ up to horizon, with local contrast compressed by `30%–70%` and chroma reduced by `25%–60%`.
2. Activate at least `3` of the **5 Monocular Depth Cues** in every deep-space shot ([Block, The Visual Story](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.routledge.com%2FThe-Visual-Story-Creating-the-Visual-Structure-of-Film-TV-and-Digital-Media%2FBlock%2Fp%2Fbook%2F9781138014152)):
   - **Contour Overlap (Interposition)**: Foreground elements partially occlude `5%–20%` of secondary midground/background shapes (avoiding edge-to-edge tangents).
   - **Relative Size / Perspective Convergence**: Place recognizable reference forms at $1\times$, $2\times$, and $4\times$ depth so perspective foreshortening ($\text{screen height} \propto 1/Z$) establishes world scale.
   - **Aerial / Atmospheric Perspective**: Apply exponential height/depth fog $T(d) = \exp(-\sigma_t \cdot d)$ with extinction coefficient $\sigma_t \in [0.005, 0.04]\text{ m}^{-1}$, shifting distant surfaces toward the sky/ambient scattering color ($L \in [0.55, 0.80]$, cool hue $h \in [205^\circ, 245^\circ]$, low chroma $C \le 0.06$) ([Birn, Digital Lighting and Rendering](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2F3drender.com%2Flight%2Findex.html)).
   - **Depth of Field (Defocus)**: Apply physically parameterized Circle of Confusion (CoC) blur proportional to $|Z - Z_{\text{focus}}| / Z$.
   - **Motion Parallax**: Translate the camera laterally (`truck` or `orbit`) rather than purely rotating (`pan/tilt`) so foreground elements sweep across the screen `2.5×–6.0×` faster than background elements ($\omega_{\text{screen}} \propto v_{\text{cam}} / Z$).
     Event mapping: Scene layout hierarchy grouping elements into `layer_fg`, `layer_mg_hero`, and `layer_bg`, paired with `atmosphere_event(density_sigma, height_falloff, inscatter_oklch)` and `camera_dof_event(focus_distance_z, f_stop)`.
     Raster/bake note: Approximating volumetric atmospheric scattering without ray marching: evaluate analytical exponential height fog + directional Henyey-Greenstein phase function ($g \in [0.6, 0.85]$) per pixel in the depth-buffer post-pass, or use a froxel (frustum-aligned voxel) `160×90×64` compute grid. **Quality loss vs. path tracing**: analytical depth fog lacks volumetric multi-scattering and sharp god-ray occlusion shafts unless paired with a low-res screen-space radial occlusion or froxel shadow-map lookup.
     Cultural notes: Orson Welles and Gregg Toland (_Citizen Kane_) pioneered deep-focus (`f/8–f/16`) multi-plane staging where FG, MG, and BG remain simultaneously razor-sharp; modern commercial 3D motion design heavily favors shallow-DoF (`f/1.4–f/2.8`) macro-photography staging with creamy foreground bokeh `[OPINION]`.
     Failure modes: Placing a 3D mesh against an empty infinite plane with no FG or BG reference objects and a locked nodal pan → zero parallax and zero scale cues; allowing a background vertical edge to align exactly with the hero character's head contour ("false attachment tangent") → destroys depth separation.
     Checklist: [ ] Scene organized into explicit FG (`0.25–0.6× Z`), MG (`1.0× Z`), and BG (`≥ 2.5× Z`) planes [ ] At least 3 depth cues active (overlap, scale, atmosphere, DoF, parallax) [ ] Atmospheric perspective shifts distant Z toward lower contrast and cooler/desaturated OKLCH [ ] Camera moves include lateral translation (`truck`/`dolly`) to activate parallax [ ] Zero false-attachment tangents across Z-planes
     Sources: [Block, The Visual Story](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.routledge.com%2FThe-Visual-Story-Creating-the-Visual-Structure-of-Film-TV-and-Digital-Media%2FBlock%2Fp%2Fbook%2F9781138014152); [Brown, Cinematography: Theory and Practice](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.routledge.com%2FCinematography-Theory-and-Practice-For-Cinematographers-and-Directors%2FBrown%2Fp%2Fbook%2F9780367373450); [Birn, Digital Lighting and Rendering](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2F3drender.com%2Flight%2Findex.html).

### 8.2 Motivated Three-Point Lighting, High/Low-Key Ratios, and Sequencing

Definition: The directional, angular, and radiometric ratioing of Key, Fill, and Rim (Back) light sources—anchored to plausible environmental sources (motivated lighting)—to sculpt 3D form and establish mood ([Birn, Digital Lighting and Rendering](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2F3drender.com%2Flight%2Findex.html); [Brown, Cinematography: Theory and Practice](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.routledge.com%2FCinematography-Theory-and-Practice-For-Cinematographers-and-Directors%2FBrown%2Fp%2Fbook%2F9780367373450)).
Why:

- Emotion: Key-to-Fill contrast ratio is the single strongest driver of dramatic tension: Low-Key (`8:1` to `16:1+`) evokes mystery, danger, and intimacy; High-Key (`1.5:1` to `2:1`) evokes optimism, safety, and clarity ([Brown, Cinematography: Theory and Practice](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.routledge.com%2FCinematography-Theory-and-Practice-For-Cinematographers-and-Directors%2FBrown%2Fp%2Fbook%2F9780367373450)).
- Attention: The brightest specular and diffuse wrap of the Key and Rim lights defines the exact surface planes the viewer's eye locks onto first.
- Production value: Separating the three visible planes of an object into distinct luminance steps (`100%` Key plane : `35%–50%` Fill plane : `12%–20%` Core Shadow + `120%–200%` Rim grazing highlight) creates tangible 3D solidity ([Birn, Digital Lighting and Rendering](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2F3drender.com%2Flight%2Findex.html)).
- Clarity: A grazing Rim/Kicker light cuts the subject's dark shadow side clean away from dark backgrounds.
- Cost: In a GPU raster pipeline, limiting real-time shadow-casting dynamic lights to `1–2` per hero subject (Key + optional Rim) and folding Fill into spherical harmonics / ambient probes maximizes frame rate.
  Rules:

1. Configure **Three-Point Angular Geometry** relative to the camera-to-subject axis ($0^\circ$ azimuth, $0^\circ$ elevation):
   - **Key Light**: Azimuth `30°–45°` (Rembrandt/Loop lighting) or `60°–75°` (Side/Split dramatic lighting), Elevation `+25° to +45°` ([Birn, Digital Lighting and Rendering](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2F3drender.com%2Flight%2Findex.html); [Brown, Cinematography: Theory and Practice](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.routledge.com%2FCinematography-Theory-and-Practice-For-Cinematographers-and-Directors%2FBrown%2Fp%2Fbook%2F9780367373450)).
   - **Fill Light**: Opposite side of camera at Azimuth `-35° to -60°`, Elevation `+5° to +20°`, with soft source angular diameter `≥ 3×` the Key light's angular diameter; **never enable hard shadows on a Fill light** (dual crossing shadow edges read as artificial stage Mistakes) ([Birn, Digital Lighting and Rendering](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2F3drender.com%2Flight%2Findex.html)).
   - **Rim / Back / Hair Light**: Azimuth `135°–165°` behind the subject (opposite the Key), Elevation `+30° to +60°`, grazing the silhouette contour at `0.8×–2.5×` Key intensity.
2. Lock the **Key-to-Fill Ratio** ($\text{Key}+\text{Fill} : \text{Fill}$ in linear radiometric exposure stops) to the scene's genre and emotional target ([Brown, Cinematography: Theory and Practice](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.routledge.com%2FCinematography-Theory-and-Practice-For-Cinematographers-and-Directors%2FBrown%2Fp%2Fbook%2F9780367373450)):
   - **High-Key (Comedy, Commercial Product, Kids, UI)**: `1.5:1` to `2:1` (`0.6–1.0 stop` difference), bright background (`L ∈ [0.65, 0.90]`).
   - **Naturalistic / Narrative Standard**: `3:1` to `4:1` (`1.5–2.0 stops` difference).
   - **Low-Key / Chiaroscuro (Thriller, Luxury Tech, Noir, Drama)**: `8:1` to `16:1+` (`3.0–4.0+ stops` difference), dark background (`L ∈ [0.05, 0.22]`) with `60%–80%` of frame pixels below middle gray (`0.18` linear).
3. Enforce **Color Temperature Opposition** between Key and Fill: if the Key light is warm daylight/tungsten (`3200K–5200K`, $h \approx 55^\circ\text{–}75^\circ$), tint the Fill light toward cool sky/shadow (`6500K–9000K`, $h \approx 220^\circ\text{–}250^\circ$, $C \in [0.03, 0.08]$), or vice versa ([Birn, Digital Lighting and Rendering](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2F3drender.com%2Flight%2Findex.html)).
4. **Sequential Light Reveal Choreography**: For hero product or title reveals in dark low-key environments, stagger light ignition events rather than turning all lights on at once: (1) Rim Light sweeps across silhouette (`t = 0`, `duration = 18–30f`) → (2) Fill / Environment Probe fades up (`t = +12f`, `duration = 20f`) → (3) Key Light + Specular sweep ignites (`t = +20f`, `duration = 24f`, `cubic-out`) `[OPINION]`.
   Event mapping: `light_rig_event(subject_id, key_azimuth=40, key_elev=35, key_intensity=4.0, key_kelvin=4800, key_to_fill_ratio=4.0, fill_kelvin=7500, rim_azimuth=150, rim_intensity=5.0)`.
   Raster/bake note: In a GPU rasterizer without ray-traced area lights, approximate soft area-light penumbras using **Percentage-Closer Soft Shadows (PCSS)** with `16–32` Poisson-disk blocker/PCF samples and Linearly Transformed Cosines (LTC) for polygonal area-light specular reflections. **Quality loss vs. path tracing**: PCSS can exhibit banding or contact-hardening artifacts at extreme penumbra widths (`> 15%` shadow map UV span), which must be masked with temporal blue-noise jitter.
   Cultural notes: Classical Hollywood studio portraiture (Paramount/Butterfly and Rembrandt lighting) established the `45°` Key triangle on the shadow cheek; contemporary luxury hardware commercials (smartphones, watches) use animated rectangular LTC area-light cards ("light-sweep rigs") that glide across curved bevels to trace form via specular reflections rather than diffuse lighting `[OPINION]`.
   Failure modes: Placing the Key light at `0°` azimuth/elevation directly on the camera axis ("flat frontal flash") → destroys all surface normal gradients ($N \cdot L \approx 1.0$ everywhere) and makes a 3D mesh look like a flat 2D cutout; casting two distinct hard shadows from Key and Fill → looks like a cheap multi-bulb stage set.
   Checklist: [ ] Key light positioned at `30°–75°` azimuth, `+25°–45°` elevation [ ] Key-to-Fill ratio explicitly set (`2:1` high-key, `4:1` natural, `8:1+` low-key) [ ] Fill light has zero hard shadow casting and opposite color temperature to Key [ ] Rim light separates dark subject edges from dark backgrounds [ ] PCSS + LTC area specular used for soft raster light sources
   Sources: [Birn, Digital Lighting and Rendering](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2F3drender.com%2Flight%2Findex.html); [Brown, Cinematography: Theory and Practice](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.routledge.com%2FCinematography-Theory-and-Practice-For-Cinematographers-and-Directors%2FBrown%2Fp%2Fbook%2F9780367373450).

### 8.3 Baked Global Illumination, Lightmaps, Light Probes, and Ambient Occlusion

Definition: The offline precomputation of indirect bounce lighting, sky irradiance, glossy environment reflections, and crease shadowing into static UV lightmaps, Spherical Harmonics (SH) / tetrahedral light probe volumes, cubemap reflection probes, and Ambient Occlusion (AO) textures.
Why:

- Emotion: Indirect bounce light and color bleeding unify objects with the ground plane and environment, creating warmth, realism, and tactile presence ([Birn, Digital Lighting and Rendering](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2F3drender.com%2Flight%2Findex.html)).
- Attention: Contact Ambient Occlusion anchors objects physically to surfaces so the viewer's eye accepts the spatial illusion without distraction.
- Production value: Missing contact shadows and un-occluded glowing crevices ("floating object syndrome") are the #1 giveaway of un-baked, low-budget raster 3D.
- Clarity: Preserves surface form definition inside direct-light shadow regions via directional irradiance rather than flat constant ambient terms.
- Cost: Offline light baking shifts 100% of multi-bounce global illumination cost to pre-production asset compilation, enabling path-traced-quality indirect lighting in a `< 2 ms` GPU raster pass.
  Rules:

1. **Static Geometry → Directional UV Lightmaps**:
   - Generate a dedicated, non-overlapping **Second UV Channel (`UV1`)** for all static architecture and environment meshes with a minimum inter-island gutter padding of `≥ 4 texels` at target lightmap resolution (`e.g., 4/1024 = 0.0039 UV units`) to prevent bilinear mipmap light bleeding across seams ([Blender Reference Manual](https://www.google.com/search?q=https%3A%2F%2Fdocs.blender.org%2Fmanual%2Fen%2Flatest%2F)).
   - Set **Lightmap Texel Density** to `16–32 texels per world meter` for hero midground surfaces, `8–12 texels/meter` for secondary surfaces, and `2–4 texels/meter` for distant background geometry `[OPINION]`.
   - Bake **Directional Lightmaps** (storing both irradiance RGB and dominant incoming light direction via Spherical Harmonics L1 or Half-Life 2 basis vectors) rather than flat scalar lightmaps, so normal maps still receive directional bump shading inside indirect shadow zones.
2. **Dynamic / Moving Meshes → Light Probes & Reflection Probes**:
   - Sample indirect diffuse irradiance on moving meshes from a 3D tetrahedral or grid **Light Probe Volume** encoded as **Order-2 (L2) Spherical Harmonics** (`9 coefficients × 3 RGB channels = 27 floats per probe`), spaced every `1.0–2.5 meters` near hero paths.
   - Sample indirect specular reflections from parallax-corrected (box-projected or sphere-projected) **Pre-convolved Cubemap Reflection Probes** (`256×256` to `512×512` per face in `BC6H` / `fp16`, mip-chain pre-filtered for GGX roughness $\alpha \in [0.0, 1.0]$ via split-sum approximation).
